Climate Change Introduction

Assigned Background

A Storm is Brewing - Is it Climate Change?

A Storm is Brewing. Is it Climate Change?

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2022

Series: How to Save a Planet

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

For years, we were told it wasn’t possible to link specific weather events to global warming — and that made communicating about climate change difficult: When extreme weather events were in the news, climate change was often left out of the story. This week, we tell the story of how that changed. We talk to the scientists who figured out how to model the role of climate change in events like Hurricane Harvey — the climate detectives who blew the case of extreme weather wide open.Guests: Dr. Peter Stott, UK Met Office; Dr. Friederike (Fredi) Otto, Grantham Institute for Climate Change and the Environment; Dr. Stephanie Herring, NOAACalls to Action: Help out climate scientists from your couch! Climate scientists rely on accurate weather data to produce and test their models. But lots of historical weather data is still locked away in handwritten records from the 1800s, like old ships’ logs. Now, you can help rescue that data — by transcribing old temperature and weather logs! Check out the project Weather Rescue, which connects volunteers with documents that need transcribing. Especially good if you’re a pro at deciphering cursive handwriting… Talk about the weather! If an extreme weather event linked to climate change occurs in your region, talk to your friends and family about how climate change is affecting the weather they’re experiencing. One goal of scientists like Fredi Otto is to help get climate science into the conversation — and that only works if people HAVE that conversation. People like you! Further Reading: You can find more information and recent studies on extreme weather events online at World Weather Attribution Check out Dr. Fredi Otto’s book about the development of rapid event attribution, Angry Weather, or Dr. Peter Stott’s memoir about his experiences as a climate scientist, Hot Air The American Meteorological Society publishes an annual round-up of the most interesting extreme weather event attribution studies, edited by Drs. Stephanie Herring and Peter Stott. If you liked this episode, you might like some of these past shows: Unnatural Disasters and How TV Weathercasters Went from Climate Skeptics to Champions. If you take an action we recommend in one of our episodes, do us a favor and tell us about it! We’d love to hear how it went and what it felt like. Record a short voice memo on your phone and send it to us via our Listener Mail Form. We might use it in an upcoming episode. Find all the actions we’ve recommended on our show here!Credits:How to Save a Planet is a Spotify original podcast and Gimlet production. It’s hosted by Alex Blumberg.This episode was produced by Rachel Waldholz with help from Daniel Ackerman, Hannah Chinn and Nicole Welch. The rest of our reporting and producing team are Kendra Pierre-Louis and Anna Ladd.  Our supervising producer is Katelyn Bogucki. Our editor is Caitlin Kenney. Sound design and mixing by Peter Leonard and Lonnie Ro with original music by Peter Leonard, Emma Munger, and Billy Libby.Our fact-checker this episode is Claudia Geib.Special thanks to Dr. Stephanie Herring at NOAA for explaining a lot of this science to us so that we could explain it to you! Podcasts

A call for redesigning American streets

September 6, 2018: A call for redesigning American streets

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2018

Series: Better Off

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In this week’s podcast we’ll explore how America’s streets can be redesigned to benefit bicyclists and pedestrians—and we’ll explain why doing so may even help mitigate the effects of climate change. A new study from Anne Lusk, research scientist in the Department of Nutrition at Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health, analyzed how bicyclists and pedestrians responded to various placements of trees along cycle tracks—barrier-protected bicycle-exclusive paths between the road and the sidewalk. The research shows that the placement of these trees can play an important role in how people walking and biking perceive traffic, pollution, heat, and even safety. But the benefits extend beyond just those using the street—and could even be a factor in mitigating the effects of climate change. These trees may make it more likely that people will bike—helping to reduce pollution. And increased greenery can also help cool cities, which often suffer from a heat island effect, which means they’re significantly warmer than more rural areas. Lusk says all of this highlights the need to re-think how we’re designing streets to encourage more people to walk and bike instead of drive.

An Expert Vegetable Breeder On Innovating Crops For The Future

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2024

Series: Science Friday

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

It’s become clear to farmers and home gardeners alike that climate change is affecting the gardening landscape, literally. The climate is warming, pests are moving into different regions, and there’s a growing need for vegetable varieties that are resilient to the stresses of this new age.In the world of organic farming, the job of creating those new varieties falls to a plant breeder: someone who, often painstakingly, crosses plants until they create a new variety. Dr. Jim Myers, one of the most accomplished plant breeders in the country, has lots of experience with this.Myers created the Indigo Rose tomato, a strikingly purple variety with the same antioxidants as blueberries. He also created the green bean cultivar predominantly used by Oregon producers, and is debuting two new varieties of low-heat habanero peppers next month, dubbed “Mild Thing” and “Notta Hotta.”Myers joins Ira Flatow from Corvallis, Oregon, where he’s a professor of agricultural science at Oregon State University. They discuss his decades-long career in plant breeding and what he sees as the biggest challenges for the plant breeders of the future. Subscribe to this podcast. Follow our show on Instagram, TikTok, Facebook, and Bluesky @scifri and sign up for our newsletters. Got a science question that’s keeping you up at night? Call us: 877-472-4374 Hosted by Simplecast, an AdsWizz company. See pcm.adswizz.com for information about our collection and use of personal data for advertising.

Are extreme wildfires the new normal?

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2026

Series: Short Wave

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

With parts of the U.S, Canada, Spain and France all ablaze with wildfires, you might be wondering: Are wildfires on the rise? If so, know that you mind-melded with the entire Short Wave staff. We had the exact same thought – and then went down some reporting rabbit holes and turned it into a whole episode. And let us just say: The answer is surprisingly complicated! But don’t you worry. We get into all the nuances this episode.Interested in more science behind the headlines? Email us your question at shortwave@npr.org.Support public media with NPR+ and enjoy perks for over 25 podcasts like this one. It includes perks like bonus episodes, early access, archive access, curated playlists and sponsor-free listening. Learn more at plus.npr.org. See pcm.adswizz.com for information about our collection and use of personal data for sponsorship and to manage your podcast sponsorship preferences.NPR Privacy Policy

Transcript

REGINA BARBER: Hey, Short Wavers. It’s Gina here with a request– follow us on whichever app you’re listening from. We love sharing cool and important science stories with you. And following us means you’ll always know when our next episode is ready, just for you. And guess what. The next episode is ready.

[MUSIC PLAYING]

EMILY KWONG: You’re listening to Short Wave from NPR.

BARBER: Hello, Short Wavers, Regina Barber here with my co host, NPR science correspondent Nate Rott. Hey, Nate.

NATHAN ROTT: Hello, Gina.

BARBER: So, Nate, over the years, you’ve reported a ton on wildfires and fought some in a previous life, I heard.

ROTT: Yes, a previous, younger, more lithe life. Yes, that is correct.

BARBER: Yeah. We’re all getting old, Nate.

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: And Nate, it seems like a lot of the world is experiencing them right now. Like, fires are happening in France and Spain. Canada and even Oregon is fighting dozens of fires right now. So, with all of your expertise, riddle me this.

ROTT: OK.

BARBER: Is it my imagination, or are wildfires increasing globally?

ROTT: Gina, are you sitting down?

BARBER: I am, actually. You can see me.

ROTT: OK, yeah, I can. And you are sitting. I just wanted to confirm for anybody who can’t see you. I say, are you sitting, because overall, there are fewer wildfires globally and less land burning on average than in the past. But–

BARBER: Huh.

ROTT: –within those two sentences, Gina, there is so much nuance, and so, we’ll get into that.

ZEKE HAUSFATHER: There’s a lot of different regional trends that are underneath that.

ROTT: Yeah, so that’s Zeke Hausfather. He’s a climate scientist in California. And he says that, for example, in North American forests, like where we are, the trend is definitely more burning.

HAUSFATHER: The typical fire burns about three times more area than it did 30 years ago or 40 years ago, which is largely driven by changes in conditions. The vegetation is drier, and so when a spark catches, it’s more likely to burn a larger area.

ROTT: And Gina, globally, extreme wildfire activity has more than doubled in the last two decades.

BARBER: That’s so much.

ROTT: Yeah, it’s a lot, which means that in certain areas, there are more fires burning bigger and hotter, which, in some cases, means more smoke from those fires can travel a very long way and make a beautiful sunset, but muddy up a perfectly normal vacation.

BARBER: Yeah, which is why we’ve been seeing smoke here on the East Coast, right?

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: And one thing I’ve been wondering when it gets hazy, especially having a kid, is, like, how much time can I spend outside? And should I even be outside at all? Like, I know that there’s, like, an air quality index, but the guidelines seem vague.

ROTT: [CHUCKLES] Vague is a very diplomatic way of putting it, Gina. And–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –there’s a reason for that. You know, scientists just don’t have a lot of long-term data on wildfire smoke exposure to give people definitive guidelines, which leaves a lot of people like you and me wondering how to go about their days when there’s a lot of smoke in the air.

[MUSIC PLAYING]

BARBER: So today on the show, what to do when the world’s on fire.

ROTT: We talk about the changing wildfire landscape, what’s driving the trends, and what you can do about it.

BARBER: You’re listening to Short Wave, the science podcast from NPR.

BARBER: OK, Nate, so you said earlier that wildfires are actually decreasing globally. This is just wild to me. Like, how is that possible?

ROTT: Yeah, I mean, OK, so when we say wildfires, that includes lots of different types of environments and vegetation– forests, yes, but also, you know, grasslands and shrublands and savanna. And as anyone who messed around with fireworks as a kid or had a little pyro streak maybe like me, you will know that fire burns a lot faster and quicker in lighter vegetation, like grasses, than it does in, like, a forest, right?

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: So most of the area–

BARBER: Yeah, definitely.

ROTT: –that burns every year are grasslands, shrublands, and savannas, not forests.

BARBER: OK.

ROTT: And so, the reason that, globally, there’s less land burned is largely because there’s less grasslands and shrublands and savannas burning mostly in Africa, which, you know, is a really big continent. So that brings down the global scale.

BARBER: I love this. This is, like, fascinating, these nuances. OK, so, now, there’s these two pretty bad forest fires in France and Spain. And–

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: –outside of these fires, is Europe another place where there are fewer fires recently?

ROTT: Well, honestly, Gina, this is, like, a really active area of research, like what are the trends in wildfire are in Europe. But when our producer–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –Berly McCoy spoke to Zeke Monday about the current fires, he said the area burned was higher than average for the European Union, so–

BARBER: OK.

ROTT: –not record-setting yet. Of course, that could change. But he said these fires are understandably getting a lot of media attention because there are two big fires burning at the same time near a lot of–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –people. So it might seem like a trend, but it might not necessarily be.

BARBER: So, I mean, could this be just, like, a normal fire year for Europe? I remember, like, four years ago, a big fire in Spain. Like, is this–

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: –just normal?

ROTT: Yeah, I mean, look, it could be. But, you know, Zeke pointed out that there is a trend that is related to many wildfires today in Europe and North America everywhere. The fires that are burning in Spain and France both coincided with an extreme heat wave.

BARBER: OK, I see where this is going.

ROTT: Yeah, climate change– don’t know if you’ve heard of it, small thing–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –happening globally.

BARBER: Yeah, the climate crisis, it’s like this cartoon villain, right? It’s very predictable.

ROTT: Yeah, well, unlike a generic cartoon villain, you know, it’s not easily vanquished by Captain Planet and his gang of trusted friends.

BARBER: Yes.

ROTT: Zeke says that rising temperatures nurture fires that might otherwise just die out. So, like, take Canada, for example.

HAUSFATHER: So, every one degree centigrade, or roughly two degrees Fahrenheit, of warming during the fire season in Canada– so May to September– is associated with an 80% increase in the amount of area burned that year.

BARBER: 80%– that’s a huge jump for just, like, a couple of degrees Fahrenheit.

ROTT: Yeah, it’s massive. And he says, since the 1960s, the fire season has warmed up about four degrees Fahrenheit on average. So–

BARBER: Wow.

ROTT: –that translates to a huge amount of burned area in Canada because when it’s warmer, things just dry out quicker, so there’s literally more fuel for the fire.

BARBER: So doesn’t this also have to do with humans not doing, like, prescribed burns to clear the underbrush?

ROTT: In the United States, yes, that’s definitely true. So, in many parts of the country, not just the Western US, forests are adapted to fire. In many cases, they need it ecologically. And, you know, Native communities used fires for centuries to forests, but since at least the 1930s, we’ve been suppressing forest fires, putting them out as quickly as we can.

BARBER: I mean, Nate, only you can prevent forest fires.

ROTT: Yes, God bless Smokey Bear. It’s not Smokey the Bear, Gina. It’s Smokey Bear. Don’t let the Forest Service come after you.

BARBER: I had no idea, honestly.

ROTT: Yeah. And look, like, Smokey’s slogan was great to protect people and homes and, you know, maybe some timber, but it’s not so great to keep fuel levels down in those forests.

HAUSFATHER: When fires do occur in those forests, there’s so much undergrowth that’s accumulated that the fires are much more catastrophic. They reach up into the canopy of the trees. They become what we call crown fires that actually kill the trees–

BARBER: Geez.

HAUSFATHER: –rather than what historically has happened in Western forests, where you have these low-level fires that kill the undergrowth, but don’t reach up and actually kill the trees. The boreal forest is a different beast.

ROTT: Yeah, and the boreal forests in Canada, the ones that are smoking up parts of the US right now, those have never needed prescribed burning in the same way.

HAUSFATHER: The boreal forest–

BARBER: Wow.

HAUSFATHER: –in Canada is 4 and 1/2 times the size of Texas. And so, historically–

BARBER: What?

HAUSFATHER: –when fires burn in those forests, they’re just allowed to burn themselves out. You know, they haven’t had the same history of fire suppression that we have in the US that has built up fuel.

ROTT: So what he’s kind of saying is, it is normal for these big fires to happen in Canada, but what’s not normal is how often these huge fires are happening.

BARBER: So, historically, how often is normal for them to burn?

ROTT: So, typically, in Canada’s western boreal forest, Zeke says big fires would happen, you know, once every 50 to 200 years.

BARBER: That is not happening right now.

ROTT: No, it is much more frequent. And–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –when we’re talking about boreal forests, you know, for you non-ecology nerds out there, you know, we’re talking about these dense forests that stretch across the Northern Hemisphere, and temperatures are rising at higher latitudes faster than they are closer to the equator.

HAUSFATHER: What we’re seeing now is considerably more frequent fires, because these areas that used to be much wetter– you know, much of the the northern boreal resembles a swamp– are now much drier because of higher temperatures.

ROTT: And it’s not necessarily that more fires are starting, you know, which, in both the US and Canada, is often caused by lightning, sometimes caused by people. It’s that a warmer climate is creating the conditions for fires to get going and burn a lot more vegetation when they do.

BARBER: OK, so I grew up in the Pacific Northwest, and there weren’t a lot of wildfires. And then I had my own kid, and then wildfires and smoke were just part of the summers. And honestly, I thought I was going to just, like, avoid all that and move to the East Coast.

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: And once I’m here, I’m not going to have any of that smoke.

ROTT: Yeah.

BARBER: But for listeners in the same boat as me– like, now, I’m inhaling this smoke– like, what are the recommendations for staying healthy?

ROTT: Well, so the biggest piece of advice I could give you, Gina, is to monitor the air quality to figure out your exposure to risk. So, in the US–

BARBER: OK.

ROTT: –there’s an air quality index for how polluted–

BARBER: Yeah.

ROTT: –the air is in a certain area. You know, it’s based on how many pollutants are in the air, and that includes particulate matter, these tiny little particles that are so small, they can get into your bloodstream.

BARBER: Oh, my gosh.

ROTT: You know, there’s been a lot of research that shows that it’s particularly risky for pregnant people. So I’d highly recommend people use AQI as kind of a quick pulse check to figure out how good the air quality is. It goes from green, where the air pollution isn’t a risk to people, all the way up to maroon, which means it’s a risk for everybody. And there is limited guidance for, you know, when you should limit outdoor activity, for example. But Lisa Patel, a pediatrician and environmental scientist at Stanford Medicine Children’s Health, says it may not be a good enough tool for wildfires.

LISA PATEL: These air quality guidelines were built off the burning of fossil fuels. There was a study that was done on children between zero to five that found that wildfire smoke is about 10 times as toxic. 10 times as many children showed up to the emergency room with respiratory complaints.

ROTT: And it’s not just kids, right? Like, inhaling that smoke can lead to inflammation, which is linked to cancer, to heart and lung disease, and to dementia.

BARBER: Oof. So, what else can we do to limit our exposure to wildfire smoke?

ROTT: Yeah, so spending more time inside, you know, though, I should say, you know, staying inside won’t totally keep you from being exposed, especially in older homes with a lot of drafts. But, you know, staying inside is one piece of advice– wearing a mask when you go outside, recirculating the air in your car, you know, so you’re not sucking air directly from outside into the car. And using an air filter indoors goes a long way. Lisa says you can make one with a HEPA filter duct taped to a box fan. I’ve actually been to places where they’re doing this in Missoula, where I grew up. They are creating them for people that can’t afford air filters in their house. And when it comes to this bigger, you know, staying inside part that we’re talking about, Lisa knows that advice might be tough to hear.

PATEL: I’m a parent myself. My kids were 2 and 5 when– and we were in the middle of a pandemic. What we are in is a situation where– and it is sad for me to say. I grieve about it when I have to say it, but we’re increasingly having periods of time where it’s not safe for our kids to be outdoors.

ROTT: But she also says this is part of our new normal, which is what I also heard from Zeke.

HAUSFATHER: Like, we’re not going to be able to put the fire genie back in the bottle and return to the climate we had in the 1980s, at least not anytime in the next few centuries. So, it’s not a good news story.

BARBER: Geez.

HAUSFATHER: But I also don’t want to let people off the hook, you know. We need to adapt–

BARBER: Wow.

HAUSFATHER: –to this world of more and more extreme fires.

ROTT: You know, but both Zeke and Lisa emphasize the thing that people can do is reduce their emissions, right, which is going to stop the problem from getting even worse. You know, so that means things like switching to renewable energy, regenerative farming practices, and, you know, making more urban green space to cool down cities. You know, but sadly, we all should settle into this new normal, too.

BARBER: And more inside time, I guess.

ROTT: Yeah.

[MUSIC PLAYING]

BARBER: Nate, thank you so much for bringing us this reporting.

ROTT: Yeah, happy to do it.

BARBER: This episode was produced and co-reported by Berly McCoy. It was edited by our showrunner, Rebecca Ramirez, and fact-checked by Tyler Jones.

ROTT: The audio engineer was Robert Rodriguez. Special thanks to science correspondent, Alejandra Borunda, for her guidance. I’m Nate Rott.

BARBER: And I’m Regina Barber. Thank you so much for listening to Short Wave from NPR.

Black Gold - The Future of Food We Throw Away

Black Gold: The Future of Food…We Throw Away

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2022

Series: Gastropod

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

For a few weeks in 1987, trash was temporarily headline news: a barge filled with waste that would no longer fit in New York City’s overflowing landfills spent months wandering up and down the East Coast with nowhere to dump its smelly, rotting cargo. The trash barge’s travels triggered a long overdue public rethink of the wisdom of sending all of our waste to landfills—including food. But fast forward more than thirty years, and food still takes up more space in American landfills than anything else. About 30 to 40 percent of food produced in the US gets thrown away, rather than eaten. What’s more, putting all that rotting food inside landfills produces a lot of methane, a powerful greenhouse gas. Our ancestors knew exactly what to do with food waste; the earliest descriptions of composting were written on clay tablets more than 4,000 years ago. So why didn’t the GarBarge kick off a composting craze? And why is it so hard for us to keep food waste out of landfills? This episode, Gastropod visits the future of food waste: the high-tech facilities as well as the innovative policies that promise to keep our discarded food out of landfills, keep methane from escaping into the atmosphere, and turn those food scraps into something useful. Can a state the size of California really keep 75 percent of its food waste out of landfills, as it has pledged to do by 2025—and what will happen if it does? Listen in for compost blow-dryers, fruit-sticker bingo, and a lot of microbes! Catching Fire

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2024

Series: Big Picture Science

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

We have too much “bad fire.” Not only destructive wildfires, but the combustion that powers our automobiles and provides our electricity has generated a worrying rise in atmospheric carbon dioxide. And that is driving climate change which is adding to the frequency of megafires. Now we’re seeing those effects in “fire-clouds,” pyrocumulonimbus events. But there’s such a thing as “good fire.” Indigenous peoples managed the land with controlled fires, reaped the benefits of doing so, and they’re bringing them back. So after millions of years of controlling fire, is it time for us to revisit our attitudes and policies, not just with regard to combustion, but how we manage our wildfires? Guests: David Peterson - Meteorologist, U.S. Naval Research Laboratory Stephen Pyne - Emeritus professor at Arizona State University, fire historian, urban farmer, author of “The Pyrocene: How We Created an Age of Fire, and What Happens Next” Richard Wrangham - Ruth B. Moore Research Professor of Biological Anthropology at Harvard University and author of “Catching Fire: How Coooking Made Us Human” Margo Robbins - Co-founder and president of the Cultural Fire Management Council (CFMC), organizer of the Cultural Burn Training Exchange (TREX) that takes place on the Yurok Reservation twice a year, and an enrolled member of the Yurok Tribe Originally aired May 9, 2022 Featuring music by Dewey Dellay and Jun Miyake Big Picture Science is part of the Airwave Media podcast network. Please contact advertising@airwavemedia.com to inquire about advertising on Big Picture Science.     Yeah. That’s a really good question. Some of that is also about adaptation that’s already been implemented in different communities. So, you know, homes in the Southern United States might have air conditioning, for example. So the ambient temperatures might have a less of an impact until the temperature gets much more extreme. But generally speaking, once we see a combination of heat and humidity, that’s above about the ninety fifth percentile of whatever is normal for that area. That’s when we start to see these really sharp increases. And we think of that as a more extreme heat period. And we start to look at comparing increases in some of those severe cases of heat related illness around that threshold. What does the ninety fifth percentile mean? Again, it means something different if you’re in the Southern US compared with New York or Boston, for example.

  • Speaker: 000:18:15

    What are some signs people should look out for that might signal heat exhaustion or heat stroke, and what’s the difference between those two, Amrita?

  • Speaker: 300:18:24

    Yeah. So heat exhaustion is, maybe I could call it a little bit of a less extreme version of heat related illness. It’s where people start to feel tired. Your body is no longer able to sweat, which is one of the primary ways that we kind of cool off when it’s very hot outside. By comparison, heat stroke looks very similar to a regular stroke. It’s dizziness. It’s, kind of loss of speech patterns. It’s, you know, changes in your ability to speak. It’s changes in your ability in your cognitive function. And it is generally something that requires more severe treatment. So, if you have heat exhaustion, you can generally treat that at home with hydration. One of the things that the mayor was mentioning earlier is that water is life. That’s very important. If you’re having a heat stroke, that should be you know, you should be looking out for some of those more severe symptoms, and that should be a good reason to visit an emergency room or an urgent care facility to get your core body temperature cooled down.

  • Speaker: 000:19:32

    Jeva, there are as many as twelve thousand recorded heat deaths in The US each year. How does that how do we account for that? How do we know that people have died because of extreme heat?

  • Speaker: 400:19:42

    It’s a complicated question. There’s about three different ways you can calculate excess or, heat deaths. One is direct and indirect. These are two different categories of looking at deaths. So direct deaths from heat, there’s a diagnostic code. It’s x 30. It’s a medical classification for a death due to excessive natural heat. This is distinct from excessive unnatural heat, such as a tanning bed death, a little bit of morbid trivia through the morning. But the direct deaths are exactly what we’re talking about in terms of heat exhaustion or heat stroke. The the classic what you think of as somebody dying from heat. Then there are excess dust or indirect dust from heat. That’s cardiovascular events, stroke, complications from diabetes, respiratory illness, all the other ways that heat can kill you that’s maybe a little bit more invisible. So if somebody comes into a hospital with a heart attack, the physicians are treating that heart attack. They’re not sitting there being like, is this heart attack from the heat outside right now? So it might not be coded as a direct heat death, but it’s still somebody who died from the heat. And there’s two different ways you can count these excess heat deaths. So one is to look at how many more people died compared to what you would have expected for that period of time. And then for so for an example of this is, in France, there were, twenty one thousand six hundred all cause deaths between June 17 and July 2. That was their heat wave in Europe. Statistical modeling would have expected about fifteen thousand nine hundred and ten deaths based on the previous year, so that gives us an excess of about seven or, five thousand seven hundred. And then the third way of doing this is basically a predictive model. So saying we know when the temperature rises by x amount, this number of people will die and kind of using that model to fit with, the temperatures that were recorded. So there’s a bunch of different ways to record excess deaths, a bunch of different models that need to be peer reviewed. And so this this work is still ongoing for heat events, both in Europe and in North America. But this is why you can see many different numbers for how many people died in a given heat wave.

  • Speaker: 000:21:50

    That’s Jiva Lang, a staff writer at Heatmap News. We’re also talking to Amrita Nourasarmi Nuri Sarma. She’s a environmental epidemiologist studying climate change and health. We’re getting texts and emails from you all. We got this text from one of you. I am medically unable to deal well with heat. Anytime the temps start hitting the upper eighties, I am strictly inside or headed for another air conditioned space. Of course, that means I miss out on all the nice outdoor activities, including the beach. Amrita, can I ask you we also got this text? Is there any data on how COVID is impacting people’s heat tolerances or exacerbating heat effects? I’ve been getting more frequent migraines from the heat. Friends of mine with high blood pressure are reporting suffering. It seems like I’m less able to tolerate it now than I was a couple of years ago.

  • Speaker: 300:22:40

    Yeah. That’s a great question. And I think that there are potentially two things that are happening here. The first one is that, obviously, COVID changed a lot of people’s, ability to be in public spaces. And I think for a long time, people were kind of isolating and staying indoors, avoiding close contact with other people. And as a result, I think there have been some long term impacts on, immune systems across the board. And so I think people have definitely suffered from that long lack of exposure to the outdoors and to other people. And I think that has some, immunological implications for the long term. I think the other thing is that people who have experienced COVID illness, and at this point, we’ve gone through multiple waves of COVID, that might also have an individual level impact, as this person mentioned in their note. So people who have experienced maybe some of the symptoms of long COVID, we do talk about often how one of the most vulnerable groups, to extreme heat is people with preexisting medical conditions. So we do know that people who have underlying, for example, cardiovascular diseases or illnesses, people who have respiratory illnesses, people who have pre existing mental health conditions may be particularly susceptible when it’s very hot outside. And I would say the same is likely true for people who are experiencing the symptoms of long COVID. There might be some individual level vulnerability because of the long term medical impacts that you’re facing after having a period of COVID infection as well.

  • Speaker: 000:24:16

    I wanted to, Jeeva, talk to you about wildfire smoke. This year, along with the heat, we’ve seen wildfire smoke from Canada blanketing the Midwest and East Coast affecting a hundred and fifteen million Americans. What’s the relationship between extreme heat and wildfires? What’s made this year’s wildfire smoke especially bad?

  • Speaker: 400:24:35

    Yeah. So there’s, it’s there there’s a lot of relationships between wildfire smoke and heat. In particular, this year, the the smoke was directed over the most populated areas of The United States by the heat dome, which was raising all of those temperatures. So the temperatures got really hot in, Northern Minnesota and Ontario. There were lightning fires, and, that smoke then rotated around this heat dome that was making extreme temperatures, and blew the smoke into some of the most populated areas of the country. And this is, when we talk about, like, compounding climate crises, this is kind of the classic example of you wanna run your air conditioning, you wanna have your windows open because it’s so hot out, but at the same time, you need to protect yourself from the wildfire smoke. So these two crises really go hand in hand and make a kind of a perfect storm of, like, terrible conditions that you want to avoid.

  • Speaker: 000:25:34

    Yeah. Emerta, a study published in February by the journal Scientific Advance shows that wildfire smoke inhalation caused twenty four thousand deaths each year from 2006 to 2020. What effect does wildfire smoke have on our bodies?

  • Speaker: 300:25:53

    Well, we know that there are a lot of impacts from just overall air pollution exposure. There’s a pretty direct relationship between how much particulate matter you’re inhaling, especially the finer particulate matter that’s smaller in size, which can descend deeper into the lungs and cross the blood brain barrier and lead to a whole host of different health outcomes. And we know that that happens even at very low levels. So once we get into particulate matter that’s much of much higher in concentration, such as the, PM that we see from wildfires, we can see a lot of different health impacts from that. We see respiratory illnesses. We see cardiovascular illnesses. We see increases in stroke. There are a lot of different health impacts from, exposure to that high level of particulate matter. The other thing that I think is really important is, you know, in addition to people not being used to those very high levels, you know, in The United States, because PM 2.5 has historically since the Clean Air Act, been pretty heavily regulated, we’ve seen, pretty low concentrations on a regular basis of particulate matter. But when we have a wildfire where trees are burning or maybe urban areas are burning, we’ll see these huge spikes in the levels of PM 2.5, and people just aren’t used to it. People are experiencing these extreme levels, that that they haven’t been experiencing historically at baseline.

  • Speaker: 000:27:18

    Jeva, in a headline this month, heat map calls wildfire smoke the East Coast’s new forever problem. Are there federal guidelines that exist around wildfire smoke the way we’ve been talking about cities trying to deal with extreme heat? From a policy standpoint, are we doing enough to deal with this persistent problem?

  • Speaker: 400:27:38

    I don’t think so. This is such a new problem in parts of the country that there aren’t really standards for dealing with it. I think a really good example of this actually is that heat and air quality alerts are not typically considered together. These are two separate metrics and two separate alert systems. And, really, they should not be triggered independently. They should be considered together. Again, as you were talking about, they go hand in hand. And I think we’re just seeing the way that cities are kind of on their back foot with dealing with wildfire smoke when it comes to everything from deciding at what point we should cancel sporting events or outdoor events. There’s not really an AQI threshold that people talk about yet. And I think, yeah, it’s just an area that there’s a lot of opportunity to make decisions now before it gets worse.

  • Speaker: 300:41:55

Climate Change and Extreme Heat End of Summer Episode with Dr. Kelly Turner and Dr. Ladd Keith

In episode 192 of America Adapts, Doug Parsons hosts Dr. Kelly Turner and Dr. Ladd Keith. Kelly is an Associate Professor of Urban Planning at UCLA and Ladd is an assistant professor in the School of Landscape Architecture and Planning at the University of Arizona.  We learn how extreme heat disproportionately impacts marginalized communities.  We also explore the evolving media coverage of extreme heat. The three of us break down the highs and lows of all the media coverage of extreme heat this past summer.  We also learn about Kelly’s research on heat’s impact on California schools, and what that means for both public health and student learning.  We take a dive into federal approaches to heat management and learn about the glaring disparity in resources and attention allocated to extreme heat compared to other climate hazards and ponder the potential for FEMA to classify extreme heat as a disaster declaration.  We also discuss the value of having a national adaptation plan for extreme heat planning. Finally, and yes, we go there, we get an update on the efforts to name heat waves. We highlight the pros and cons of this approach and discuss other options.  It is a gangbuster episode and that’s just a sampling of what we cover. Transcripts for this episode available here. Increasing prevalence of extreme heat due to climate change and its disproportionate impact on marginalized groups. Media coverage of extreme heat is becoming more sophisticated, highlighting impacts on workers and public health. Dr. Turner’s research in California focuses on schools and heat’s impact on student learning. Discussion of heating planning strategies including shade in urban design. The Biden administration is updating heat safety labor laws, emphasizing the need for comprehensive regulations at state and federal levels and the potential for FEMA labeling heat events as disaster declarations. Naming heat waves to increase public awareness is debated, highlighting the importance of reaching vulnerable populations. Will heat lead to managed retreat from regions that become uninhabitable? A national adaptation plan is emphasized to address heat impacts, especially for marginalized communities. Designing for climate change

August 10, 2018: Designing for climate change

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2018

Series: Better Off

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

A new study from Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health found that students who lived in dormitories without air conditioning during a heat wave performed worse on a series of simple tests compared with students who lived in air-conditioned dorms. The findings show that the effects of extreme heat are not just felt by those typically thought of as vulnerable—such as the elderly. And with global temperatures on the rise, the research underscores the need for sustainable design solutions in mitigating the health impacts of extreme heat. In this week’s episode, we’ll speak with the authors of that study to learn how we can  better design our buildings and cities to adapt to our changing climate. You’ll hear from Joe Allen, assistant professor of exposure assessment science and director of the Healthy Buildings Program at the Center for Climate, Health, and the Global Environment, and Jose Guillermo (Memo) Cedeño Laurent, associate director of the Healthy Buildings Program.

Does It Feel Like Mosquitoes Are Getting Worse?

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2025

Series: Short Wave

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

Mosquitoes are the deadliest animal on the planet, and some of them may be on the rise. At least in listener Abigail Krich-Starr’s area, that’s due to warmer, wetter weather — which, yes, is linked to climate change. But it doesn’t stop there: Ecologists and entomologists say increased heat could also alter mosquito behavior, shift their natural habitat, and even change how pathogens incubate and spread inside their bodies.So how do you protect yourself against the (mosquito) masses? Our experts suggested several things:- Assess your risk by checking local mosquito surveillance efforts, like this one for the state of Massachusetts- Consider rescheduling outdoor events happening between dusk and dawn, which is peak biting time for multiple mosquito species- Wear long-sleeved shirts and pants, weather-permitting, to limit exposed skin- Use an EPA-approved DEET repellent, and/or a permethrin spray for clothing and outdoor gearThis episode is part of Nature Quest, a monthly Short Wave segment that answers listener questions about the local environment.Got a question about changes in your local environment? Fire And Ice - Linking Intense Wildfire And The Melting Arctic

Fire And Ice: Linking Intense Wildfire And The Melting Arctic

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2023

Series: Short Wave

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In the Arctic Ocean, sea ice is shrinking as the climate heats up. In the Western U.S., wildfires are getting increasingly destructive. Those two phenomena are thousands of miles apart, but scientists are uncovering a surprising connection. The ice is connected to weather patterns that reach far across North America. And as the climate keeps changing and sea ice shrinks, Western states could be seeing more extreme weather, the kind that fuels extreme wildfires.Check out the full series about how melting ice affects us all: npr.org/icemelt. Growing a Healthier Climate

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2026

Series: Humans in Public Health

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In this episode, we sit down with Professor Meredith Niles to explore how climate-friendly agriculture—from methane-reducing rice irrigation to no-till farming—is actually a quiet powerhouse for public health. While traditional environmental messaging has hit a wall, shifting the narrative to human health benefits (like keeping heavy metals out of baby food and clearing our air) might be the key to finally getting farmers, consumers and policymakers on board. It’s time to take off the carbon goggles and look at the health potential of sustainable farming.

The planet is heating up, every plan to stop it has fallen short, and growing group of scientists has started to ask a stranger question: what if we grabbed the thermostat ourselves? Ben Bradford investigates geoengineering — the science of deliberately manipulating Earth’s climate — from space mirrors and ocean fertilizers to fleets of planes mimicking a volcano. Some of it sounds like cartoon villainy. Some of it might actually work. Oropouche Virus - More than a smidge worrisome

Ep 206 Oropouche Virus: More than a smidge worrisome

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2026

Series: This Podcast Will Kill You

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

Though discovered relatively recently, Oropouche virus has been making headlines as an emerging vector-borne infectious disease on the rise. Not transmitted by the usual suspects (like ticks and mosquitoes), this virus is instead spread through the bites of midges or no-see-ums. Since these arthropods are already widely distributed and their range is growing thanks to climate change, this is a recipe for potential disaster. In this episode, we take you through the story of Oropouche virus, from how it makes us sick to what the construction of a highway has to do with its discovery, from surprising prevalence statistics to the history of One Health. Tune in for the full scoop on this midge and the virus it carries. Should I Give Up Beef?

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2022

Series: How to Save a Planet

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

A quarter of global greenhouse gas emissions come from agriculture and land use – and a big portion of those emissions come from producing meat. Adopting a plant-based diet is one of the biggest steps an individual can take to reduce their own carbon footprint. So, should we all stop eating meat? Or is it more complicated than that? This week, we take a tour through the bodily functions of cows, millions of acres of corn, and the hidden policy that shapes the American food system to answer that question once and for all.(This episode first aired March 25, 2021.)Calls to action: Sign up to track the latest U.S. Food & Agriculture bills here, and contact Agriculture Committee members (House and Senate) about supporting a climate-friendly Farm Bill. Smog Cloud Silver Lining

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2023

Series: Radiolab

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

Summer 2023 was a pretty scary one for the planet. Global temperatures in June and July reached record highs. And over in the North Atlantic Sea, the water temperature spiked to off-the-chart levels. Some people figured that meant we were about to go over the edge, doomsday. In the face of this, Hank Green (a long time environmentalist and science educator behind SciShow, Crash Course, and more), took to social media to put things in context, to keep people focused on what we can do about climate change. In the process, he came across a couple studies that suggested a reduction in sulfurous smog from cargo ships may have accidentally warmed the waters. And while Hank saw a silver lining around those smog clouds, the story he told—about smog clouds and cooling waters and the problem of geoengineering—took us on a rollercoaster ride of hope and terror. Ultimately, we had to wrestle with the question of what we should be doing about climate change, or what we should even talk about.Special thanks to Dr. Colin Carson and Avishay Artsy. Sudden Flood in Tibet - The 340th Evolutionary Lens with Bret Weinstein and Heather Heying

Sudden Flood in Tibet: The 340th Evolutionary Lens with Bret Weinstein and Heather Heying

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2026

Series: DarkHorse Podcast | Bret Weinstein and Heather Heying

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

On this, our 340th Evolutionary Lens livestream, we discuss the recent flash flood on the border of Nepal and Tibet, climate science, and the nature of evidence. What commonalities are there between a glacial dam breaking and flooding the valley below, taking out towns and villages; Dry Falls and the Columbia River Gorge in Washington state; the Boxing Day tsunami in the Indian Ocean; and the 2011 earthquake that resulted in the destruction of the Fukushima nuclear power plant in Japan? In some cases we have to infer what happened; in some cases we have direct observation; in all cases we have to construct a plausible story that fits all of the evidence. Meanwhile in climate science, a new paper reconstructs NOAA data from 1,200 weather stations from over 100 years, uses a different metric than NOAA does to describe the data, and concludes that the world—or at least the coterminous U.S.—is not heating up in the way we have been told. The History And Legacy Of Hurricane Helene

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2026

Series: 1A

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

On the night of Sept. 27, 2024, Hurricane Helene dropped a historic wall of rain on the southern Appalachian mountains.Some 31 inches of water fell in three days. The Swannanoa River rose 27 feet. Roads, bridges, and entire communities – very little was left in the storm’s aftermath.  More than 250 people died across the Southeast. More than a hundred of them in North Carolina alone. It was the deadliest hurricane to hit the continental United States since Katrina.We check in on how much western North Carolina has rebuilt in the nearly two years since the storm. Ticks are a growing problem, no matter where you live

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2026

Series: Short Wave

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

In the grand scheme of things, Lyme disease is a fairly new scientific discovery. It was first traced back to ticks in the late 70s and early 80s. The tick-borne illness can cause a rash, fever, pain, neurological complications, and even facial paralysis. It’s spread by only two of the nearly 50 species of ticks in the United States. Historically, most Lyme cases were limited to a small region, including the Great Lakes area and northeastern US. But thanks to changing temperatures, animal migration and shifts in land use, scientists say tick territory is expanding. So what does that mean for Lyme disease risk? And do you have to be worried about it in your own backyard?Interested in more episodes about pests and parasites? What Ever Happened to the Ozone Hole?

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2026

Series: Are We Doomed?

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

A dark blue wound across the bottom of the world — that’s what NASA images showed in the 1980s. It was a hole in the ozone layer, eight million square miles across. Human activity and convenience had quietly, but rapidly, eaten away  Earth’s crucial shield against the sun, one that likely helped make life on land possible in the first place.Ben Bradford goes back to re-examine the story of the ozone hole: how people discovered it, how they fought over what to do, and what finally happened. It’s a tale of hairspray, human progress, exploding refrigerators, scientists balancing on icy Antarctic rooftops, and Ronald Reagan’s ranch. And from it, maybe, we can find a map for building consensus on our most pressing environmental challenges today.Guest:Susan Solomon, MIT atmospheric scientist and one of the key figures in solving the ozone crisis.Support Are We Doomed?, get bonus episodes, and more: https://doompod.com/support/ Watch earlier episodes on Youtube! https://www.youtube.com/@arewedoomedpod To manage podcast ad preferences, review the links below:See pcm.adswizz.com for information about our collection and use of personal data for sponsorship and to manage your podcast sponsorship preferences. Open Series in Spotify

Dotted across the Great Basin of the American West are salty, smelly lakes. The largest of these, by far, is the Great Salt Lake in Utah.But a recent report found that water diversions for farming, climate change and population growth could mean the lake essentially disappears within five years. Less water going in means higher concentrations of salt and minerals, which threatens the crucial ecological role saline lakes play across the West, as well as the health of the people who live nearby. On today’s episode, Kirk takes Short Wave co-host Aaron Scott on an audio field trip to the endangered Great Salt Lake, and explains why losing the lake could be devastating for everyone from brine flies to the humans that live next door. See pcm.adswizz.com for information about our collection and use of personal data for sponsorship and to manage your podcast sponsorship preferences.NPR Privacy Policy

Transcript

EMILY KWONG, BYLINE: You’re listening to SHORT WAVE from NPR.

AARON SCOTT, HOST:

Hey, SHORT WAVErs. Time to slip on those rubber boots because we’re off for a field trip with NPR correspondent Kirk Siegler. Hey, Kirk.

KIRK SIEGLER, BYLINE: Hey, Aaron.

SCOTT: So where are you taking us today?

SIEGLER: Well, we’re going to the Great Salt Lake in Utah. I trekked out there earlier this winter with Carly Biedul. She’s a biologist with the Great Salt Lake Institute.

CARLY BIEDUL: These might even be my footprints from last week.

SIEGLER: Carly is bundled up in an orange, puffy jacket, gloves and hat. And most importantly, she’s wearing thick, sturdy, rubber boots ’cause the mud with the frozen slick layer of ice on top is treacherous.

BIEDUL: Probably not fully, fully fresh, but…

SIEGLER: But the only thing we’re not prepared for is the stench.

This is pungent right here.

BIEDUL: Yeah.

SIEGLER: Smells like dead fish, almost.

SCOTT: Oh, yes. I’ve been on similar salty lakes here in Oregon. Whew. That smell is intense and immense.

SIEGLER: It is. And the stink is actually a good thing because it’s a sign of a biologically healthy saline lake.

BIEDUL: People have been saying that they miss the lake stink ’cause it just makes it feel like home, and it’s just not here anymore. So you’re lucky that it gets to smell so bad.

SCOTT: Oh, so the smell is disappearing?

SIEGLER: It is. Because, Aaron, the Great Salt Lake is drying and dying. Biedul hikes out here weekly. She’s trying to collect samples of tiny brine fly larva, which are getting harder and harder to find.

BIEDUL: I’m going to get my jar out.

SIEGLER: So think of it, the brine flies she’s looking for are at the bottom of the food chain. They’re feed for the migrating birds and so on. And most of the water that’s left out there is too salty now.

BIEDUL: The threshold is - we’re kind of at the threshold. So if things get any saltier, we’re super, super worried.

SIEGLER: And they may have good reason. A recent report says climate change, farming and population growth in Utah may dry up the lake in five years.

SCOTT: So today on the show - the future of the Great Salt Lake and similar lakes across the West. Why are they drying up? And what’ll we lose if they do? I’m Aaron Scott, and you’re listening to SHORT WAVE, the daily science podcast from NPR.

(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

SCOTT: OK, Kirk. So to begin, I’d love it if you would just paint us a picture of the Great Salt Lake, for those who’ve never had the pleasure of seeing it. What does it look like, and how did a big, salty lake end up so far from the ocean?

SIEGLER: Well, it - I can’t even overstate how large it is. It’s huge. And especially in the winter, it’s quite eerie. When we were out there, it was foggy and otherworldly and very, very shallow. In fact, we - you know, you heard there, trekking out in the mud where there should have been water because the lake is receding. So to answer your question, these saline lakes occur at the bottom of basins where there’s no way for the water to flow out to the sea. So basically, in Utah, when all the snow off the Wasatch Mountains right by Salt Lake City melts, it pools into the rivers, into these lakes in the spring and then they evaporate over the summer, leaving all sorts of particles behind and minerals, including saline.

SCOTT: And as you said, I mean, these saline lakes occur all across the Great Basin in the West. And they - they’re actually essential ecosystems, right?

SIEGLER: Exactly. They’re very important for migrating birds at the top of the food chain. And so when Carly Biedul is out there looking for larva at the bottom of the food chain, that’s an indicator of a potential ecological collapse. And I’m not being dramatic. These lakes occur in the Great Basin from Utah to Nevada, across to eastern California, even in parts of Oregon, as you say. There’s this huge geological anomaly. And a lot of these lakes have dried up over time. Some naturally, but a lot due to water diversions. Utah’s Wasatch Front, where the majority of the state’s population lives along Salt Lake City, is one of the fastest-growing places in the country. So water is in high demand. But also, just like across the West, most of the water being diverted out of the rivers that would be going to the Great Salt Lake are being diverted for farming and, in particular in Northern Utah, alfalfa farming in the high desert.

SCOTT: So water diversions is playing a huge role in why they’re disappearing. I’m guessing climate change also plays a role?

SIEGLER: Yes, because we’re in the West and - I don’t have to tell you - we’re in the midst of a 23-year megadrought. So drought and climate change are a big factor.

SCOTT: And so if these lakes are essential stopovers for birds as they migrate from Alaska and Canada down to South America, I mean, just millions of birds kind of stopping here, like this rest stop all-you-can-eat buffet, what’s it going to mean for them if these lakes dry up?

SIEGLER: Well, it’s another species that we know will be in peril. This is an essential flyover spot for migratory birds, and the effects could be devastating.

SCOTT: And it’s not just the birds, of course. I mean, there’s a lot of creatures around the lakes that depend on them, including us humans. What’s it going to mean for us?

SIEGLER: Well, one of the most immediate impacts may be pollution. This is a dried-up lake bed. And we’ve seen this in other parts of the West. Dust storms off of a dried-up lake bed that has toxins in it - naturally occurring toxins - blowing into a large population base will have untold impacts on health. And the Salt Lake City area, particularly in the winter, already has some of the dirtiest air in the nation as a result of natural inversions that occur in that basin, but also a lot of cars and a lot of other industries. So that’s the most immediate impact. But then there are also trickle-down effects, as well. There’s tourism. There’s mining that depends on the lake, and Utah’s famous ski resorts actually benefit from getting more snow from lake effect storms. So the impacts are countless, to be honest.

SCOTT: I mean, given all of that, Kirk, I’m guessing there’s probably a lot of outcry and activism and such going on by people living near the lake, right?

SIEGLER: There is. This is a huge topic that’s all over the news these days.

(SOUNDBITE OF ARCHIVED RECORDING)

UNIDENTIFIED NEWS ANCHOR #1: Utah still remains in a drought.

UNIDENTIFIED NEWS ANCHOR #2: Now to what some tonight are calling a looming environmental nuclear bomb in Utah. The mighty Great Salt Lake is drying up and…

UNIDENTIFIED NEWS ANCHOR #3: As the Great Salt Lake continues to shrink, a…

SIEGLER: They don’t. And he’s particularly concerned there because these neighborhoods in the West Valley - they’re more working class. They’re already hemmed in by two busy freeways. There’s a planned freeway expansion. Salt Lake City International Airport is right there and Utah’s largest oil refinery.

BITTON: I mean, we’re talking about something that could potentially make these neighborhoods - I don’t want to say uninhabitable - but for those that are vulnerable, for those that have lung issues, uninhabitable.

SCOTT: Is he being a little dramatic there, Kirk?

SIEGLER: He really isn’t. You know, researchers have found higher rates of asthma and cardiovascular disease in neighborhoods like these. And one University of Utah study even showed that students in schools around there scored lower on tests during bad air days. Dr. Brian Moench is president of Utah Physicians for a Healthy Environment. He says the state should have declared an emergency years ago.

BRIAN MOENCH: A lot of people think that dust is pretty benign because it’s, quote, \“natural.\” Well, that’s not the case. And in the case of dust from the Great Salt Lake, it is particularly toxic because we know that it’s laced with high concentrations of heavy metals.

SIEGLER: And most of Utah’s 3 million or so residents live just east of the lake along the Wasatch Mountains. Now, the lake is about 9 feet lower than normal right now, despite a good winter. And locals are already complaining of these dust storms.

SCOTT: OK, Kirk, so a lot of people upset about this. What’s being done about it? Is there any sort of movement in, like, the state legislature?

SIEGLER: There is. And there are a lot of promises so far, but it’s been remarkable in this, you know, politically divided time in a Republican-controlled state, how - in particular, how there’s bipartisan agreement over trying to fix the lake. There was a lot of pressure on lawmakers to do that. Some ideas floated, I have to say, sounded a bit like sci-fi - cloud seeding, even a pipeline to pump Pacific Ocean water in. Not exactly the same thing…

SCOTT: Wow.

SIEGLER: …But everything seems to be on the table. Lawmakers this past session debated setting a target elevation for the lake and also a half-billion-dollar package that would do things like pay farmers and cities to use less water. Here’s Republican Governor Spencer Cox in his recent State of the State address.

(SOUNDBITE OF SPEECH)

SPENCER COX: Earlier this month, a report predicted that in just five short years, the Great Salt Lake will completely disappear. Let me be absolutely clear - we are not going to let that happen.

SCOTT: Wow. Strong words. So did they pass any of those measures?

SIEGLER: Sort of? You know, they didn’t pass any big water use regulations or major targets for the lake. Now, they did include money in the budget for more water conservation, but they took out language from a bill that would have required the conserved water actually make it to the lake. The federal government, though, is also a player here, and they’re clearly paying attention. In the final days of last year, you had President Biden signing a bipartisan bill called the Saline Lake Ecosystems in the Great Basin States Program Act. Yeah.

SCOTT: That is a mouthful.

SIEGLER: Right. Well, but what you need to know - it allocates $25 million for the U.S. Geological Survey to work with other agencies - local land managers, scientists and nonprofits - to study and monitor these saline lakes in the Great Basin and come up with a conservation plan. But, you know, there’s a real sense of urgency here with the Great Salt Lake if, in fact, it could dry up in five years.

SCOTT: So the idea is we need more information before we can come up with a plan.

SIEGLER: Yeah. But, you know, in large part, I think it’s going to come down to the states because they manage the water rights. Last year, the Utah legislature did approve some money to try to buy up water rights. But pointing to the Salt Lake Tribune, they’ve been reporting that no farmers have actually taken them up on it. And this is a thing across the West right now, although we did get news just this week that one powerful player in Utah who you might have heard of - The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints - they’re getting involved. They’ve announced that they will permanently donate 20,000 acre feet of their water rights to the lake. So it’s something.

SCOTT: I think I hear those little brine fly larvae rejoicing just a little bit.

SIEGLER: You can. Wow. That’s good. So we don’t have to end on such a doom and gloom note. And I have to tell you, down at the lake, trekking out there with Carly Biedul, the biologist - she’s privy to all these negative headlines, and she goes out there every week. But she is just trying to stay positive herself.

BIEDUL: And that’s kind of what we’ve been trying to do - is find these moments to see the beauty when it’s so sad.

SIEGLER: It is beautiful out there. But Biedul says it’s sad because there’s not much time left to save the Great Salt Lake.

SCOTT: Thank you, as always, for bringing this to us, Kirk.

SIEGLER: You’re welcome.

What Worsening Floods Mean For Superfund Sites

Duration: = this.duration_string

Released: 2024

Series: Science Friday

Open Episode in Spotify

Open Series in Spotify

Superfund sites are some of the most polluted areas in the country, containing highly toxic waste such as asbestos, lead, and dioxin. Cleaning them up, which follows a systematic, science-based process as required by law, can take decades.There are more than 1,300 of these sites across the US, from Florida’s Panhandle to the banks of the Rio Grande in New Mexico. They’re found in nearly every state, often near residential areas. The EPA estimates that 78 million people live within three miles of a Superfund site—nearly 1 in 4 Americans.But these waste dumps face a growing threat: the worsening effects of climate change. The EPA has determined that more than 300 Superfund sites are at risk of flooding. The actual number of flood-prone sites, however, may be more than twice that amount, according to a 2021 Government Accountability Office report. Floodwaters can move toxic waste into neighboring communities, which threatens drinking water, agriculture, and broader ecosystem health.Read more at sciencefriday.comTranscripts for each segment will be available after the show airs on sciencefriday.com. Why are scientists planting tiny forests in big cities?

Duration: = this.duration_string

Healthy forests help combat climate change, provide humans with drinking water and even improve mental and physical health. But it’s hard to imagine an entire forest in the middle of a big city. That’s where micro-forests come into play — public forests on a smaller scale, filled with native plants. They exist around the world, and producer Rachel Carlson went to visit the largest micro-forest in California in this encore episode. Why Heat Waves Become Deadly

Open Series in Spotify

Today we’re sharing an episode of The Carbon Copy, a climate change podcast produced by Canary Media. The topic is extremely timely: heat waves. This summer saw extreme heat blanketing almost every region of the northern hemisphere. And these heat waves aren’t just uncomfortable or inconvenient. They’re deadly. In most years, extreme heat kills more people in the U.S. than any other weather-related disaster. This episode asks: how can we better prepare for these heat waves? Climate Change Resilience Cooling an Urban Heat Island

American Museum of Natural History, 08 11, 2018

Video (1:42)

Description

Cities tend to be much warmer than their surrounding areas, and global warming is exacerbating this heat island effect. Painting roofs white helps cool buildings and lower energy demand. In New York City, installing these reflective rooftops supports the City’s goal to reduce carbon emissions by 80 percent by 2050.

0:02 · COMMISSIONER GREGG BISHOP: The “urban heat island” effect affects a lot of cities like New York. Because a tremendous amount of buildings, a lot of concrete, and little vegetation increases the overall temperature of the city. And when the city is really, really, really hot, it affects the health of our citizens, individuals who may suffer from asthma, or may be susceptible to heat waves, our elderly population. So, heat actually affects life and safety.

0:31 · One of the strategies that we have to combat the urban heat island effect is the NYC CoolRoofs initiative, which works simply by painting the roofs white.

0:39 · BRIDGET CARTER: See how the coating is going inside the seams?

0:42 · Put it on as heavy as you can get it.

0:44 · BISHOP: Many buildings, because of the black roof, absorb heat. And painting them white reduces the internal temperature of a building by 10-30 percent.

0:53 · CARTER: Remember guys, don’t stretch the coating.

0:55 · Don’t stretch the coating.

0:56 · Where is your bucket?

0:57 · That reflective coating is going to keep the building cooler, conserve energy for the people that live in the building— less air conditioning and less fans being run. People can save some money.

1:07 · Very energy efficient.

1:08 · BISHOP: This CoolRoofs program is really a part of the Mayor’s strategy to lower New York City’s emissions by 80 percent by 2050, and to really help New York City become a great partner in fighting climate change.

Drowning in seaweed How to stop invasive Sargassum

Description

The story of Sargassum stretches from the Congo to the Amazon, from soybean farming to climate change and from toxic gases to robotic vehicles. In this documentary researchers from around the world explain the vast scope of the Sargassum problem and the solutions that may one day begin to address it.

Additional photographs and footage in this film were provided by Drake Lee-Patterson, Gary Marshall, Joseph Weekes, Driven Visual Media, Kerwin Noel, Nick Hurley, Akeisha Clark, Sargassum Information Hub, NASA, Chuanmin Hu, Jeff Davis’s team, Hazel Oxenford, Caroline Juang, Melvina Walsh, Morgan Lewis, Kerton Jobe, P McConney, Markel Gomez Letona and Nico Frohberg who were mistakenly missed off the film credits.

6:46 · VOICE OVER: Sargassum sequesters a lot of carbon, but it also releases it when it decays.

6:52 · This approach would theoretically remove the carbon from the atmosphere, effectively buying the planet time in the race against climate change.

6:59 · AJIT: Sinking down 2,000 metres, our current model suggests that that particular water body does not come back in contact with the atmosphere for at least 100 years.

7:08 · VOICE OVER: The idea of sinking the sargassum has been proposed before, but the suggestion is usually to sink it where it grows, in shallower water.

7:15 · AJIT: Merely capturing it at the surface and getting rid of it just off the beach at 100 metres or something like that will basically have the carbon coming right back up.

7:26 · It might even actually come back up in a more potent form if you are starting to have it rotting and forming methane.

7:32 · VOICE OVER: Ajit plans to sink the sargassum in deeper water, but there are a lot of questions still to be answered.

7:38 · AJIT: We want to make sure that the carbon that we are capturing is actually going down to the sea floor and will stay down there, and so we want to follow it down to the sea floor.

7:46 · VOICE OVER: In addition, as a biologist, he’s keen to understand the impact of this plan on the ecosystem.

7:51 · AJIT: So, we actually have a fisheries scientist on the team who will be looking at what kind of communities the sargassum rafts harbour, and therefore we’ll have a sense of what we might be impacting if we were to do the sinking.

8:02 · It’s not that we’re going to decimate the ocean of sargassum.

8:06 · VOICE OVER: The list of problems attributed to sargassum is growing. From the harmful gases released when it decays to its impact on wildlife.

8:13 · HARSHINI: Especially when it washes up on beaches where turtles go to lay their eggs, a lot of these baby turtles are actually unable to climb over these sargassum things, and so they’re unable to make it into the ocean due to these big patches of sargassum.

8:28 · SHELLY-ANN: So, every year we’re seeing more countries reporting the influx and the catastrophic impacts on tourism, fisheries sectors, maritime and transport and then the health impacts.

8:43 · So, some of our colleagues recently published a paper on the impacts on pregnant women who were exposed to hydrogen sulfide and ammonia when the sargassum decomposes.

8:57 · Those pregnant ladies who live near the coast and were exposed were at a higher risk of preeclampsia.

9:04 · VOICE OVER: Going into the future, it’s likely that the challenge presented by sargassum, like many of the challenges of climate change, might be bigger than one scientist or one idea.

9:14 · HARSHINI: There needs to be some sort of economically driven, or at least be governmentally subsidised, but there needs to be some sort of way to like tackle and solve the problem.

9:27 · AJIT: I feel that countries like Barbados are the populations that contribute the least to climate change and yet are facing the greatest impact of climate change.

9:36 · I would like to figure out a financing model where we can actually have credit going to communities in Barbados for the carbon that is sequestered.

9:46 · SHELLY-ANN: There are lots of bright, brilliant minds out there developing solutions that need this support.

9:53 · They need the financial support to upscale until governments realise that this problem is really here to stay, then they will be willing then to invest in building up sargassum industries.

10:07 · HARSHINI: Nothing has like had any concrete potential yet. There’s just several isolated research groups.

10:15 · But I just think that things would move a lot faster if everyone could like work together.

Extreme Heat Is the New Normal

Extreme Heat Is the New Normal Op-Docs

New York Times Opinion, 08 28, 2026

Video (15:42)

Description

We filmed “A New Inferno,” the short documentary above, in Phoenix, one of the hottest cities in America, through some of the city’s hottest days of 2025, during one of the hottest years on record globally. On one of our production days, temperatures reached 118 while we were filming outside.

In the face of extreme heat, we documented firefighters and paramedics of the Phoenix Fire Department saving lives in real time. We wanted to call attention to a specialized technique of ice bag immersion for people facing life-threatening heat stroke. We hope that telling their story and showcasing the efficacy of this strategy will encourage wider adoption of this protocol across the country.

Yet this procedure is ultimately a stopgap, as firefighters do their best to adapt to the climate crisis in front of them. The underlying story of heat in Phoenix is one of broader, interlocking crises: homelessness and substance use disorders that are deeply entangled with urban heat, each amplifying the other, illustrating how climate change affects the disenfranchised most brutally.

With our film, we aim to reframe how the public thinks about heat: as an invisible, silent killer reshaping the rhythms of urban life.

Documentary and text by By Nita Blum-Reddick and Jonathan Pickett

More from Op-Docs: https://www.nytimes.com/column/op-docs

Subscribe: http://bit.ly/U8Ys7n

Humans are increasingly reliant on air conditioning, but its use contributes to global warming. As the world warms and demand rises, how can we make A/C sustainable? In Spain and India, Kal Penn explores two innovative solutions.
